| wendigo-c10 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Whenever a wendigo ate another person it would grow in proportion to the meal, so it could never be full. | Whenever a wendigo ate another person, it would grow in proportion to the meal it had just eaten, so it could never be full. |

| wendigo-c12 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | A wendigo need not lose the human's powers of cognition or speech, and in some depictions may communicate with prospective victims or even threaten or taunt them. | A wendigo need not lose the human's powers of cognition or speech and in some depictions may clearly communicate with its prospective victims or even threaten or taunt them. |

| wendigo-c17 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Humans could also turn into wendigos by being in contact with them for too long. | Humans could also turn into wendigos by being in contact with them for too long. |
| wendigo-c18 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Among the Assiniboine, Cree and Ojibwe a satirical ceremonial dance is sometimes performed during famine to reinforce the seriousness of the wendigo taboo. | Among the Assiniboine, the Cree, and the Ojibwe, a satirical ceremonial dance is sometimes performed during times of famine to reinforce the seriousness of the wendigo taboo. |

| anansi-c01 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Taking the role of a trickster, he is also one of the most important characters of West African, African-American and Caribbean folklore. | Taking the role of a trickster, he is also one of the most important characters of West African, African-American and Caribbean folklore. |
| anansi-c02 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Anansi is best known for his ability to outsmart and triumph over more powerful opponents through cunning, creativity and wit. | Anansi is best known for his ability to outsmart and triumph over more powerful opponents through his use of cunning, creativity and wit. |
| anansi-c03 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Anansi is depicted in many ways and under many names, from "Ananse", "Kwaku Ananse" and "Anancy" to New World iterations such as "Ba Anansi", "Kompa Nanzi", "Nanzi", "Nancy", "Aunt Nancy" and "Sis' Nancy", though he is always male in his stories. | Anansi is depicted in many different ways and with different names, from "Ananse", "Kwaku Ananse", and "Anancy", to his New World iterations, such as "Ba Anansi", "Kompa Nanzi" and/or "Nanzi", "Nancy", "Aunt Nancy", and "Sis' Nancy", even though he is always depicted as a male in his stories. |

| anansi-c05 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Anansi or Ananse (literally "spider") is a character in Akan religion and folklore associated with stories, wisdom, knowledge, wit, mischievousness, cunning and trickery, most commonly depicted as a spider. | Anansi or Ananse ... literally translates to spider) is a character in Akan religion and folklore associated with stories, wisdom, knowledge, wit, mischievousness, cunning, and trickery, most commonly depicted as a spider. |
| anansi-c06 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | The Akan are a close-knit people from present-day southern Ghana who rely on social order, which is conveyed through the stories of their culture. | The Akan people are a close-knit people from present-day southern Ghana who rely on social order, which translates through the stories that come out of their culture. |
| anansi-c07 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | In many ways Anansi is a paradoxical character whose actions defy this social order, but by incorporating rebellion and doubt into faith, his folkloric presence strengthens it. | In many ways, Anansi is a paradoxical character whose actions defy this social order, but in incorporating rebellion and doubt into faith, his folkloric presence strengthens it. |
| anansi-c08 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | These spider tales were spread to the Americas via the Atlantic slave trade. | These spider tales were spread to the Americas via the Atlantic slave trade. |

| anansi-c10 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | The tales later spread to the West Indies, Suriname, Sierra Leone (where they were introduced by Jamaican Maroons) and the Netherlands Antilles. | They later spread to the West Indies, Suriname, Sierra Leone (where they were introduced by Jamaican Maroons) and the Netherlands Antilles |
| anansi-c11 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | In several folktales Anansi has a family: his long-suffering wife Okonore Yaa (known elsewhere as Aso, Crooky or Shi Maria) and his sons Ntikuma the firstborn, Tikelenkelen the big-headed, Nankonhwea with a spindly neck and legs, and Afudohwedohwe the pot-bellied. | Anansi also has a family in several folktales involving him, consisting of his long-suffering wife Okonore Yaa - known in other regions as Aso, Crooky, or Shi Maria; Ntikuma, his firstborn son; Tikelenkelen, his big-headed son; Nankonhwea, his son with a spindly neck and spindly legs; finally, Afudohwedohwe, his pot-bellied son. |
| anansi-c12 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Anansi stories were part of an exclusively oral tradition, and Anansi himself was seen as synonymous with skill and wisdom in speech. | Anansi stories were part of an exclusively oral tradition, and Anansi himself was seen as synonymous with skill and wisdom in speech. |
| anansi-c13 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Anansi was often celebrated as a symbol of slave resistance and survival, because he is able to turn the tables on his powerful oppressors through cunning and trickery. | Anansi was often celebrated as a symbol of slave resistance and survival, because Anansi is able to turn the tables on his powerful oppressors by using his cunning and trickery |
| anansi-c14 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Anansi becomes both an ideal to aspire toward and a cautionary tale against the selfish desires that can cause our undoing. | Anansi becomes both an ideal to be aspired toward, and a cautionary tale against the selfish desires that can cause our undoing. |
| anansi-c15 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | Names for Anansi in different places include Kacou Ananzè (Côte d'Ivoire), Kwaku Ananse (Jamaica, Ghana), Bru Nansi (Virgin Islands), Anansi or Anancy (Jamaica, Grenada, Costa Rica, Colombia, Nicaragua), Anansi Drew (The Bahamas) and Aunt Nancy (South Carolina). | Kacou Ananzè (Côte d'Ivoire) Kwaku Ananse (Jamaica), (Ghana) Bru Nansi (Virgin Islands) Anansi or Anancy (Jamaica, Grenada, Costa Rica, Colombia, Nicaragua) Anansi (Trinidad and Tobago, Turks and Caicos Islands) Anansi Drew (The Bahamas) Aunt Nancy (South Carolina) |

| la-llorona-c06 | exact | blogs.loc.gov | 'La Llorona' literally means 'the weeping woman', so it is not surprising that the main characteristic shared by all stories of La Llorona is that she weeps. | “La Llorona” literally means “the weeping woman,” so it’s not surprising that the main characteristic shared by all stories of “La Llorona” is that she weeps. |
| la-llorona-c07 | exact | blogs.loc.gov | In Latin America, in Spanish-speaking communities in the U.S., and especially in Mexico, no ghost story is told, discussed, or interpreted as often or as widely as the legend of La Llorona. | In Latin America, in Spanish-speaking communities in the U.S., and especially in Mexico, no ghost story is told as often, discussed as enthusiastically, or interpreted as widely, as the legend of La Llorona. |
| la-llorona-c08 | exact | blogs.loc.gov | La Llorona is often closely associated with children. | bell-witch-c07 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | The apparition began to speak aloud; asked who it was and what it wanted, the voice answered feebly, "I am a spirit; I was once very happy but have been disturbed." | The apparition began to speak out loud and was asked, "Who are you and what do you want?" and the voice answered feebly, "I am a spirit; I was once very happy but have been disturbed." |

| bell-witch-c11 | exact | en.wikipedia.org | The supernatural powers attributed to the Tennessee spirit have also been compared to those of jinn in mythology. | The supernatural powers attributed to the Tennessee spirit have also been compared to that of jinn in mythology. |
